\end{center}

Conservation of probability flux (equivalently, constancy of the Wronskian) gives
$$
|R(k)|^2 + |T(k)|^2 = 1,
$$
which is on the first example sheet. One expects, and it is true, that $T(k)\to 1$ and $R(k)\to 0$ as $k\to\infty$: a sufficiently energetic wave barely notices the potential.

So far this is all hypothetical -- we have described what a solution would look like if it existed. Let us check that it does.

The difficulty with existence questions for differential equations is that differentiation makes functions worse: it can destroy differentiability, or even continuity, so a differential operator may fling us out of whatever function space we started in. Integration, by contrast, makes functions better. So the standard move is to convert the differential equation into an integral equation, and work there.

Consider therefore the integral equation for $f = f(x,k)$,
$$
f(x,k) = f_0(x,k) + \int_{-\infty}^\infty G(x-y,k)u(y)f(y,k)\dd y,
$$
where $f_0$ is any solution of $(\partial_x^2 + k^2)f_0 = 0$ and $G$ is a Green's function for $\partial_x^2 + k^2$, so that
$$
(\partial_x^2 + k^2)G(x,k) = \delta(x).
$$
Any solution of the integral equation solves the eigenvalue problem, since
\begin{align*}
	(\partial_x^2+k^2)f &= (\partial_x^2+k^2)f_0 + \int_{-\infty}^\infty(\partial_x^2+k^2)G(x-y,k)u(y)f(y,k)\dd y\\
	&= 0 + \int_{-\infty}^\infty\delta(x-y)u(y)f(y,k)\dd y\\
	&= u(x)f(x,k),
\end{align*}
which rearranges to $Lf = k^2f$.

To get the boundary condition we want, we choose $f_0 = e^{-ikx}$ and take the retarded Green's function
$$
G(x,k) = \begin{cases}0 & x<0,\\ \frac{1}{k}\sin(kx) & x\ge 0.\end{cases}
$$
Then the integrand vanishes unless $y \le x$, so for $x$ far to the left of the support of $u$ the integral is zero and $f(x,k) = e^{-ikx}$ exactly, as required.

It remains to show that the integral equation has a solution. Write it abstractly as
$$
(I - K)f = f_0, \qquad (Kf)(x) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty G(x-y,k)u(y)f(y,k)\dd y,
$$
so that formally
$$
f = (I-K)^{-1}f_0 = \left(I + K + K^2 + \cdots\right)f_0.
$$
This is a \vocab{Neumann series}, and one shows on the second example sheet that it converges (the point being that $G$ is bounded and $u$ has compact support, so $K$ is a contraction after finitely many iterations). Given convergence, it visibly solves the equation:
$$
(I-K)\left(f_0 + Kf_0 + K^2f_0+\cdots\right) = f_0 + Kf_0 + \cdots - \left(Kf_0 + K^2f_0 + \cdots\right) = f_0.
$$
We will use this series once more, for a completely different purpose, in Section 3.4; the key consequence to remember is that \emph{the solution with $f_0 = 0$ is $f = 0$}.

\subsubsection{Bound States}

Now take $\lambda = -\kappa^2 < 0$, and look for solutions of
$$
L\psi_\kappa = -\kappa^2\psi_\kappa
$$
which are normalisable,
$$
\|\psi_\kappa\|^2 = \int_{-\infty}^\infty\psi_\kappa(x)^2\dd x = 1.
$$
These are the \vocab{bound states}, and we take them real without loss of generality.

Again, since $u$ has compact support, any solution obeys $\varphi_{xx} - \kappa^2\varphi = 0$ for large $|x|$, so is a combination of $e^{\pm\kappa x}$ out there. Fix $\varphi_\kappa$ by
$$
\varphi_\kappa(x) = e^{-\kappa x}\quad\text{as }x\to+\infty,
$$
so that it decays to the right, and write
$$
\varphi_\kappa(x) = \alpha(\kappa)e^{\kappa x} + \beta(\kappa)e^{-\kappa x}\quad\text{as }x\to-\infty.
$$
For \emph{any} $\kappa$ we can solve the ODE and find such a $\varphi_\kappa$. What we cannot do for any $\kappa$ is make it normalisable: the term $\beta(\kappa)e^{-\kappa x}$ blows up as $x\to-\infty$, so we need
$$
\beta(\kappa) = 0.
$$
This is a genuine constraint, and it is what quantises the spectrum. One can show that $\beta$ has only finitely many zeros, which we label
$$
\chi_1 > \chi_2 > \cdots > \chi_N > 0.
$$
The corresponding normalised bound states are
$$
\psi_n(x) = c_n\varphi_{\chi_n}(x),
$$
with the constants $c_n$ chosen so that $\|\psi_n\| = 1$. Equivalently, $c_n$ is determined by the large-$x$ behaviour
$$
\psi_n(x) = c_ne^{-\chi_n x}\quad\text{as }x\to+\infty.
$$
We call the $c_n$ the \vocab{normalisation constants}.

\subsubsection{The Scattering Data}

Assembling everything, the outcome of the forward problem is the following package.

\begin{definition}[Scattering data]
	The \vocab{scattering data} of the operator $L = -\partial_x^2 + u$ is
	$$
	S = \left\{\{\chi_n, c_n\}_{n=1}^N,\ R(k),\ T(k)\right\}.
	$$
\end{definition}

Let us compute it in an example where everything can be done by hand.

\begin{example}[A delta-function potential]
	Take $u(x) = -2\alpha\delta(x)$ with $\alpha>0$. (This is not of compact support, but it decays even faster, so all is well.)

	\emph{Continuous spectrum.} Since $u$ vanishes away from the origin, we have exactly
	$$
	\Phi(x,k) = \begin{cases}T(k)e^{-ikx} & x<0,\\ e^{-ikx} + R(k)e^{ikx} & x>0.\end{cases}
	$$
	Continuity at $x=0$ gives
	$$
	T = 1 + R.
	$$
	For the second condition, integrate $L\Phi = k^2\Phi$ over $(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)$ and let $\varepsilon\to0$. The $k^2\Phi$ term contributes nothing in the limit, and we obtain the jump condition
	$$
	\Big[\Phi_x\Big]_{0^-}^{0^+} = -2\alpha\Phi(0),
	$$
	that is
	$$
	\left(-ik + ikR\right) - \left(-ikT\right) = -2\alpha T.
	$$
	Substituting $T = 1+R$ and solving the two equations gives
	$$
	R(k) = \frac{i\alpha}{k - i\alpha}, \qquad T(k) = \frac{k}{k-i\alpha}.
	$$
	As a check,
	$$
	|R|^2 + |T|^2 = \frac{\alpha^2 + k^2}{k^2+\alpha^2} = 1,
	$$
	and as $k\to\infty$ we do indeed find $R\to0$, $T\to1$.

	\emph{Bound states.} Away from the origin $-\psi_{xx} + \chi^2\psi = 0$, and decay at both ends forces
	$$
	\psi(x) = c\,e^{-\chi|x|}.
	$$
	The same jump condition now reads
	$$
	-c\chi - c\chi = -2\alpha c \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \chi = \alpha.
	$$
	So there is exactly one bound state, with $\chi_1 = \alpha$. Normalising,
	$$
	1 = c_1^2\int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{-2\alpha|x|}\dd x = \frac{c_1^2}{\alpha} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad c_1 = \sqrt\alpha.
	$$
	Hence the scattering data is
	$$
	S = \left\{\{\alpha, \sqrt\alpha\},\ \frac{i\alpha}{k-i\alpha},\ \frac{k}{k-i\alpha}\right\}.
	$$
\end{example}

The next example is the one that matters most, because it is a soliton.

\begin{example}[The soliton potential]
	Take $u(x) = -2\sech^2x$, which is the one-soliton profile with $\chi = 1$ at time $t=0$.

	\emph{Continuous spectrum.} We claim that
	$$
	f(x,k) = e^{ikx}\left(\tanh x - ik\right)
	$$
	satisfies $Lf = k^2f$. Writing $T = \tanh x$, so that $T' = \sech^2x = 1 - T^2$ and $T'' = -2TT'$,
	\begin{align*}
		f' &= e^{ikx}\left[ik(T-ik) + T'\right],\\
		f'' &= e^{ikx}\left[-k^2(T-ik) + 2ikT' + T''\right],
	\end{align*}
	and hence
	\begin{align*}
		-f'' - 2T'f &= e^{ikx}\left[k^2(T-ik) - 2ikT' - T'' - 2T'(T-ik)\right]\\
		&= e^{ikx}\left[k^2(T-ik) - 2ikT' - T'' - 2TT' + 2ikT'\right]\\
		&= e^{ikx}k^2(T-ik),
	\end{align*}
	since $-T'' - 2TT' = 2TT' - 2TT' = 0$. So $Lf = k^2f$ as claimed.

	Replacing $k$ by $-k$ gives another solution,
	$$
	g(x,k) = e^{-ikx}\left(\tanh x + ik\right),
	$$
	whose limits are
	$$
	g \to e^{-ikx}(ik - 1) \text{ as } x\to-\infty, \qquad g\to e^{-ikx}(ik+1)\text{ as }x\to+\infty.
	$$
	So the normalised Jost solution is $\varphi = g/(ik-1)$, and comparing with the definitions,
	$$
	a(k) = \frac{ik+1}{ik-1}, \qquad b(k) = 0.
	$$
	Therefore
	$$
	R(k) = 0, \qquad T(k) = \frac{ik-1}{ik+1}.
	$$
	The reflection coefficient vanishes identically! A soliton is completely transparent: waves of every frequency pass through it without any reflection at all. (Note $|T| = 1$, consistent with $|R|^2+|T|^2=1$.)

	\emph{Bound states.} We look for a normalisable solution of $-\psi_{xx} - 2\sech^2x\,\psi = -\chi^2\psi$. Trying $\psi = \sech x$, and using $(\sech x)'' = \sech x - 2\sech^3x$, we get
	$$
	-\psi_{xx} - 2\sech^2x\,\psi = -\sech x + 2\sech^3x - 2\sech^3x = -\sech x,
	$$
	so $\psi = \sech x$ is a bound state with $\chi_1 = 1$. Normalising, $\int\sech^2x\dd x = 2$, so the normalised state is $\psi_1 = \frac{1}{\sqrt2}\sech x$. Since $\sech x\sim 2e^{-x}$ as $x\to+\infty$,
	$$
	\psi_1(x)\sim\sqrt2\,e^{-x}, \qquad\text{so}\qquad c_1 = \sqrt2.
	$$
	One can check there are no others. So the scattering data is simply
	$$
	S = \left\{\{1, \sqrt2\},\ R(k) = 0\right\}.
	$$
\end{example}

\begin{definition}[Reflectionless potential]
	A potential $u$ with $R(k) \equiv 0$ is called \vocab{reflectionless}.
\end{definition}

We have just seen that the one-soliton potential is reflectionless. This is not a coincidence, and it is the key to everything: reflectionless potentials are exactly the potentials for which the inverse problem can be solved in closed form, and they will turn out to be exactly the $N$-soliton solutions.

\subsection{The Inverse Scattering Problem}

Now we go backwards. Given scattering data $S$, can we reconstruct the potential $u$ that produced it? The answer is yes, and remarkably the transmission coefficient is not even needed.

\begin{theorem}[Gelfand--Levitan--Marchenko]
	A potential $u = u(x)$ decaying rapidly as $|x|\to\infty$ is completely determined by the scattering data
	$$
	S = \left\{\{\chi_n, c_n\}_{n=1}^N,\ R(k)\right\}.
	$$
	Specifically, define
	$$
	F(x) = \sum_{n=1}^N c_n^2e^{-\chi_n x} + \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{ikx}R(k)\dd k,
	$$
	and let $K(x,y)$ be the unique solution of the \vocab{GLM equation}
	$$
	K(x,y) + F(x+y) + \int_x^\infty K(x,z)F(z+y)\dd z = 0, \qquad y > x.
	$$
	Then
	$$
	u(x) = -2\frac{\dd}{\dd x}K(x,x).
	$$
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
	Beyond the scope of this course.
\end{proof}

The GLM equation looks forbidding but is not. For fixed $x$ it is a linear integral equation in $y$, of the abstract form
$$
\vv K + \vv b + \mathcal A\vv K = 0
$$
for a known vector $\vv b$ and known linear operator $\mathcal A$. As with any linear equation, one can guess a solution and then verify it. In the reflectionless case, which is the one we shall care about, the integral equation degenerates into a finite-dimensional matrix equation and can be solved exactly.

Notice that the function $F$ has a very transparent structure: the sum records the bound states, and the integral is the Fourier transform of the reflection coefficient. If there are no bound states and no reflection, $F = 0$, hence $K = 0$, hence $u = 0$ -- reassuringly, the empty scattering data corresponds to the zero potential.

\begin{example}[Reconstructing the soliton]
	Let us check the theorem on the data we computed above,
	$$
	S = \left\{\{\chi_1, c_1\} = \{1,\sqrt2\},\ R = 0\right\},
	$$
	and see whether we recover $u = -2\sech^2x$. Here
	$$
	F(x) = c_1^2e^{-\chi_1 x} = 2e^{-x}.
	$$
	Since $F$ is a single exponential in $x$, we look for a solution of the same shape,
	$$
	K(x,y) = K_1(x)e^{-y}.
	$$
	Substituting into the GLM equation,
	$$
	K_1(x)e^{-y} + 2e^{-x}e^{-y} + \int_x^\infty K_1(x)e^{-z}\cdot 2e^{-z}e^{-y}\dd z = 0.
	$$
	Every term carries a factor $e^{-y}$, which we cancel, and the remaining integral is
	$$
	2K_1(x)\int_x^\infty e^{-2z}\dd z = K_1(x)e^{-2x}.
	$$
	So
	$$
	K_1(x)\left(1 + e^{-2x}\right) = -2e^{-x}, \qquad K_1(x) = \frac{-2e^{-x}}{1+e^{-2x}}.
	$$
	Hence, writing $w = e^{-2x}$,
	$$
	K(x,x) = K_1(x)e^{-x} = \frac{-2w}{1+w},
	$$
	and since $\dd w/\dd x = -2w$,
	$$
	\frac{\dd}{\dd x}K(x,x) = -2\cdot\frac{w'(1+w) - ww'}{(1+w)^2} = \frac{-2w'}{(1+w)^2} = \frac{4w}{(1+w)^2}.
	$$
	Therefore
	$$
	u(x) = -2\frac{\dd}{\dd x}K(x,x) = \frac{-8w}{(1+w)^2} = \frac{-8e^{-2x}}{(1+e^{-2x})^2} = \frac{-8}{(e^x + e^{-x})^2} = -2\sech^2x,
	$$
	exactly as it should be.
\end{example}

We can now go in both directions between potentials and scattering data. That, on its own, is a piece of nineteenth-century-flavoured mathematical physics. What makes it explosive is the next ingredient.

\subsection{Lax Pairs}

We need to connect the time evolution of $u$ to the time evolution of the scattering data. The mechanism is due to Peter Lax.

Recall from Section 1 that under a Hamiltonian flow, functions evolve according to
$$
\frac{\dd f}{\dd t} = \{f, H\},
$$
so $H$ `generates' the time evolution. In quantum mechanics the corresponding statement in the Heisenberg picture is
$$
i\hbar\frac{\dd L}{\dd t} = [L, H],
$$
for operators $L$. We want something of exactly this shape: an operator whose commutator with $L$ generates the time evolution of $L$.

\begin{definition}[Lax pair]
	Let
	$$
	L = a_m(x,t)\frac{\partial^m}{\partial x^m} + \cdots + a_1(x,t)\frac{\partial}{\partial x} + a_0(x,t)
	$$
	be a time-dependent self-adjoint linear operator, and write
	$$
	L_t = \dot a_m\frac{\partial^m}{\partial x^m}+\cdots+\dot a_0
	$$
	for its derivative with respect to $t$ (differentiating the coefficients only). If there is an operator $A$ with
	$$
	L_t = LA - AL = [L, A],
	$$
	then $(L,A)$ is called a \vocab{Lax pair}, and the equation $L_t = [L,A]$ is called \vocab{Lax's equation}.
\end{definition}

The reason to care is the following.

\begin{theorem}[Isospectral flow]
	Let $(L,A)$ be a Lax pair, with $A$ antisymmetric. Then the discrete eigenvalues of $L$ are independent of time. Moreover if $L\psi = \lambda\psi$ then
	$$
	L\tilde\psi = \lambda\tilde\psi, \qquad \text{where }\tilde\psi = \psi_t + A\psi.
	$$
\end{theorem}

The word \vocab{isospectral} means precisely this: the operator changes with time, but its spectrum does not.

\begin{proof}
	We assume that eigenvalues vary smoothly, so that an eigenvalue $\lambda_0$ with normalised eigenfunction $\psi_0$ at $t=0$ extends to a family $\lambda(t)$, $\psi(x,t)$ with
	$$
	L(t)\psi(x,t) = \lambda(t)\psi(x,t), \qquad \|\psi\| = 1.
	$$
	Differentiate with respect to $t$:
	$$
	L_t\psi + L\psi_t = \lambda_t\psi + \lambda\psi_t,
	$$
	so, substituting Lax's equation and using $L\psi = \lambda\psi$,
	\begin{align*}
		\lambda_t\psi &= L_t\psi + L\psi_t - \lambda\psi_t\\
		&= LA\psi - AL\psi + L\psi_t - \lambda\psi_t\\
		&= LA\psi - \lambda A\psi + L\psi_t - \lambda\psi_t\\
		&= (L-\lambda)\left(\psi_t + A\psi\right).
	\end{align*}
	Now take the inner product with $\psi$ and use self-adjointness of $L - \lambda$:
	$$
	\lambda_t = \ip{\psi}{\lambda_t\psi} = \ip{\psi}{(L-\lambda)(\psi_t + A\psi)} = \ip{(L-\lambda)\psi}{\psi_t + A\psi} = 0,
	$$
	since $(L-\lambda)\psi = 0$. So $\lambda$ is constant, and the displayed equation reduces to $(L-\lambda)\tilde\psi = 0$.
\end{proof}

So far this is abstract. The whole thing turns on being able to find, for a given equation, an operator $A$ making Lax's equation equivalent to it. For KdV such an $A$ exists, and here it is.

\begin{proposition}[Lax pair for KdV]
	Let
	$$
	L = -\partial_x^2 + u(x,t), \qquad A = 4\partial_x^3 - 3\left(u\partial_x + \partial_x u\right).
	$$
	Then $A$ is antisymmetric, and
	$$
	L_t = [L,A] \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad u_t + u_{xxx} - 6uu_x = 0.
	$$
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
	Throughout, $\partial = \partial_x$, and we treat everything as operators acting on a test function; in particular $\partial u$ means the operator $\psi\mapsto (u\psi)_x$, so that
	$$
	\partial u = u\partial + u_x.
	$$
	Hence
	$$
	A = 4\partial^3 - 3u\partial - 3\left(u\partial + u_x\right) = 4\partial^3 - 6u\partial - 3u_x.
	$$
	For antisymmetry, recall $\partial^\dagger = -\partial$ and $u^\dagger = u$, so
	$$
	A^\dagger = -4\partial^3 - 3\left(u\partial\right)^\dagger - 3u_x = -4\partial^3 + 3\partial u - 3u_x = -4\partial^3 + 3u\partial + 3u_x - 3u_x,
	$$
	giving $A^\dagger = -4\partial^3 + 6u\partial + 3u_x = -A$ once we recombine (the middle term arises twice, once from each half of $u\partial + \partial u$).

	Now for the commutator. We will repeatedly use the operator identities
	$$
	\partial^2 u\partial = u\partial^3 + 2u_x\partial^2 + u_{xx}\partial, \qquad
	\partial^2 u_x = u_x\partial^2 + 2u_{xx}\partial + u_{xxx},
	$$
	$$
	\partial^3 u = u\partial^3 + 3u_x\partial^2 + 3u_{xx}\partial + u_{xxx},
	$$
	each of which is just the Leibniz rule. First,
	\begin{align*}
		LA &= \left(-\partial^2 + u\right)\left(4\partial^3 - 6u\partial - 3u_x\right)\\
		&= -4\partial^5 + 6\partial^2u\partial + 3\partial^2u_x + 4u\partial^3 - 6u^2\partial - 3uu_x\\
		&= -4\partial^5 + 6\left(u\partial^3 + 2u_x\partial^2 + u_{xx}\partial\right) + 3\left(u_x\partial^2 + 2u_{xx}\partial + u_{xxx}\right)\\
		&\qquad + 4u\partial^3 - 6u^2\partial - 3uu_x\\
		&= -4\partial^5 + 10u\partial^3 + 15u_x\partial^2 + 12u_{xx}\partial - 6u^2\partial + 3u_{xxx} - 3uu_x.
	\end{align*}
	Similarly,
	\begin{align*}
		AL &= \left(4\partial^3 - 6u\partial - 3u_x\right)\left(-\partial^2 + u\right)\\
		&= -4\partial^5 + 4\partial^3u + 6u\partial^3 - 6u\partial u + 3u_x\partial^2 - 3uu_x\\
		&= -4\partial^5 + 4\left(u\partial^3 + 3u_x\partial^2 + 3u_{xx}\partial + u_{xxx}\right) + 6u\partial^3\\
		&\qquad - 6\left(u^2\partial + uu_x\right) + 3u_x\partial^2 - 3uu_x\\
		&= -4\partial^5 + 10u\partial^3 + 15u_x\partial^2 + 12u_{xx}\partial - 6u^2\partial + 4u_{xxx} - 9uu_x.
	\end{align*}
	Every differential term cancels in the difference, and we are left with the multiplication operator
	$$
	[L,A] = LA - AL = \left(3u_{xxx} - 3uu_x\right) - \left(4u_{xxx} - 9uu_x\right) = -u_{xxx} + 6uu_x.
	$$
	On the other hand $L_t = u_t$, since only the coefficient $u$ depends on $t$. So Lax's equation $L_t = [L,A]$ says exactly
	$$
	u_t = -u_{xxx} + 6uu_x,
	$$
	which is KdV.
\end{proof}

This is the miracle on which everything rests. It deserves a moment's contemplation: a fifth-order operator equation collapses, after everything cancels, to a scalar equation -- and that scalar equation happens to be KdV. Combined with the isospectral flow theorem, we already know something remarkable.

\begin{corollary}
	If $u(x,t)$ evolves according to KdV, then the discrete eigenvalues $-\chi_n^2$ of the operator $L = -\partial_x^2 + u(x,t)$ are constant in time. In particular the number $N$ of bound states never changes.
\end{corollary}

Since the $\chi_n$ are the soliton amplitudes and speeds, this already tells us that solitons cannot be created or destroyed by the evolution.

\subsection{Evolution of the Scattering Data}

We now let $u = u(x,t)$ evolve by KdV and work out how the rest of the scattering data moves. Since $u$ has compact support, note first that
$$
A = 4\partial_x^3 \quad\text{as } |x|\to\infty.
$$

\subsubsection{Continuous Spectrum}

For each fixed $t$ construct, as in Section 3.1, the solution $\varphi$ of $L\varphi = k^2\varphi$ with
$$
\varphi(x,t) = \begin{cases}e^{-ikx} & x\to-\infty,\\ a(k,t)e^{-ikx} + b(k,t)e^{ikx} & x\to+\infty.\end{cases}
$$
Here $k$ is fixed: solutions exist for every real $k$ regardless of $u$, so there is no question of $k$ moving.

Exactly as in the proof of the isospectral flow theorem (but now with $\lambda = k^2$ constant by fiat rather than by conclusion), differentiating $L\varphi = k^2\varphi$ gives
$$
0 = (L - k^2)\left(\varphi_t + A\varphi\right),
$$
so $\tilde\varphi = \varphi_t + A\varphi$ is another solution of $L\tilde\varphi = k^2\tilde\varphi$. We now compute its asymptotics, using $A = 4\partial_x^3$ out there:
$$
\tilde\varphi(x,t) = \begin{cases}
	4ik^3e^{-ikx} & x\to-\infty,\\
	\left(a_t + 4ik^3a\right)e^{-ikx} + \left(b_t - 4ik^3b\right)e^{ikx} & x\to+\infty,
\end{cases}
$$
since $\partial_x^3e^{\mp ikx} = \pm ik^3e^{\mp ikx}$.

Here is the clever step. Consider
$$
\theta = 4ik^3\varphi - \tilde\varphi.
$$
By linearity $L\theta = k^2\theta$, and by construction $\theta\to 0$ as $x\to-\infty$. But we showed in Section 3.1 that the solution of $Lf = k^2f$ with prescribed behaviour $f_0$ at $-\infty$ is $f = (I + K + K^2+\cdots)f_0$; with $f_0 = 0$ this gives $f = 0$. Hence
$$
\theta \equiv 0, \qquad\text{that is}\qquad \tilde\varphi = 4ik^3\varphi.
$$
Comparing coefficients of $e^{-ikx}$ and $e^{ikx}$ as $x\to+\infty$,
\begin{align*}
	a_t + 4ik^3a &= 4ik^3a,\\
	b_t - 4ik^3b &= 4ik^3b,
\end{align*}
that is $a_t = 0$ and $b_t = 8ik^3b$. These we can solve:
$$
a(k,t) = a(k,0), \qquad b(k,t) = b(k,0)e^{8ik^3t},
$$
and therefore
$$
\boxed{R(k,t) = R(k,0)e^{8ik^3t}, \qquad T(k,t) = T(k,0).}
$$

Stop and appreciate this. We assumed that $u$ evolves by a thoroughly nasty nonlinear PDE, and concluded that its reflection coefficient simply rotates in the complex plane at a constant rate, and that its transmission coefficient does not move at all.

\subsubsection{Discrete Spectrum}

By the isospectral flow theorem the $\chi_n$ are constant. For each $t$ we have normalised bound states $\psi_n(x,t)$ with
$$
L\psi_n = -\chi_n^2\psi_n, \qquad \|\psi_n\| = 1, \qquad \psi_n(x,t) = c_n(t)e^{-\chi_n x}\ \text{ as } x\to+\infty.
$$
By the isospectral flow theorem again,
$$
\tilde\psi_n = \partial_t\psi_n + A\psi_n
$$
also satisfies $L\tilde\psi_n = -\chi_n^2\tilde\psi_n$. Since the eigenvalue $-\chi_n^2$ is simple (a standard Wronskian argument shows bound states of a one-dimensional Schr\"odinger operator are non-degenerate), $\tilde\psi_n$ must be proportional to $\psi_n$. But
\begin{align*}
	\ip{\psi_n}{\tilde\psi_n} &= \ip{\psi_n}{\partial_t\psi_n} + \ip{\psi_n}{A\psi_n}\\
	&= \frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\ip{\psi_n}{\psi_n} + \ip{\psi_n}{A\psi_n}\\
	&= 0,
\end{align*}
because $\|\psi_n\|$ is constant and $A$ is antisymmetric (so $\ip{\psi}{A\psi} = -\ip{A\psi}{\psi} = -\ip{\psi}{A\psi}$). A multiple of $\psi_n$ orthogonal to $\psi_n$ is zero, so
$$
\tilde\psi_n \equiv 0.
$$
Now look at the large-$x$ behaviour. There $A = 4\partial_x^3$ and $\psi_n = c_n(t)e^{-\chi_nx}$, so
$$
\tilde\psi_n(x,t) = \left(\dot c_n - 4\chi_n^3c_n\right)e^{-\chi_nx}\quad\text{as }x\to+\infty.
$$
Since $\tilde\psi_n\equiv 0$ we must have $\dot c_n = 4\chi_n^3c_n$, and hence
$$
\boxed{c_n(t) = c_n(0)e^{4\chi_n^3t}.}
$$

\subsubsection{The Transform}

We can now assemble the machine. Suppose $u$ evolves by KdV with initial data $u_0(x) = u(x,0)$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=latex]
\small
\node (a) at (0,2.7) {$u_0(x)$};
\node (b) at (7.3,2.7) {$S(0) = \left\{\{\chi_n, c_n(0)\}_{n=1}^N,\ R(k,0)\right\}$};
\node (c) at (0,0) {$u(x,t)$};
\node (d) at (7.3,0) {$S(t) = \left\{\{\chi_n, c_n(0)e^{4\chi_n^3t}\}_{n=1}^N,\ R(k,0)e^{8ik^3t}\right\}$};
\draw[->] (a) -- node[above,font=\scriptsize,align=center] {scattering\\problem} (b);
\draw[->] (b) -- node[right,font=\scriptsize,align=left] {evolve the\\scattering data} (d);
\draw[->] (d) -- node[below,font=\scriptsize,align=center] {solve the\\GLM equation} (c);
\draw[->,dashed] (a) -- node[left,font=\scriptsize,align=right] {KdV\\equation} (c);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

That is: compute the scattering data of $u_0$; evolve it by the explicit formulae above; feed the result into the GLM equation; and read off $u(x,t)$. Compare this with the Fourier transform diagram at the start of this section -- it is the same three steps, with a more sophisticated transform. All the nonlinearity of KdV has been absorbed into the (nonlinear, but classical) map $u\mapsto S$.

The one thing that makes it work is the equivalence
$$
u_t + u_{xxx} - 6uu_x = 0 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad L_t = [L,A],
$$
so the whole method hinges on finding a Lax pair.

\begin{remark}
	This is not special to KdV. Choosing different operators $A$ in the Lax equation with the same $L$ produces an infinite hierarchy of equations -- the \vocab{KdV hierarchy} -- all of which are solvable by the same transform and all of which share the same conserved quantities. Choosing a different $L$ altogether produces different integrable equations; the choice
	$$
	L = i\begin{pmatrix}1 & 0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}\partial_x + \begin{pmatrix}0 & q\\ \bar q & 0\end{pmatrix}
	$$
	leads, by the same route, to the nonlinear Schr\"odinger equation, which we meet in Section 5.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Reflectionless Potentials and \texorpdfstring{$N$}{N}-Solitons}

Now we cash in. The GLM equation is hard in general, but there is one case in which it can be solved completely, and it is exactly the case that produces solitons.

Recall that a potential is reflectionless if $R(k,0) = 0$. Since
$$
R(k,t) = R(k,0)e^{8ik^3t},
$$
a potential which starts reflectionless stays reflectionless forever. So this is a class of initial data preserved by the KdV flow, and within it the machinery becomes elementary.

Suppose then that $R \equiv 0$, so that
$$
F(x) = \sum_{n=1}^Nc_n^2e^{-\chi_nx}.
$$
(We suppress the time dependence for the moment, restoring it at the very end.) Since $F$ is a finite sum of exponentials, we look for $K$ of the same shape,
$$
K(x,y) = \sum_{m=1}^NK_m(x)e^{-\chi_my};
$$
one shows on the second example sheet that $K$ \emph{must} have this form. Substituting into the GLM equation,
$$
\sum_{m=1}^NK_m(x)e^{-\chi_my} + \sum_{n=1}^Nc_n^2e^{-\chi_nx}e^{-\chi_ny} + \sum_{n,m}c_n^2K_m(x)\int_x^\infty e^{-\chi_mz}e^{-\chi_n(z+y)}\dd z = 0.
$$
The integral evaluates to
$$
\int_x^\infty e^{-(\chi_n+\chi_m)z}\dd z = \frac{e^{-(\chi_n+\chi_m)x}}{\chi_n+\chi_m},
$$
so, collecting everything over the common index $n$,
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{n=1}^N\left[c_n^2e^{-\chi_nx} + K_n(x) + \sum_{m=1}^N\frac{c_n^2K_m(x)}{\chi_n+\chi_m}e^{-(\chi_n+\chi_m)x}\right]e^{-\chi_ny} = 0.\tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
The functions $e^{-\chi_ny}$, $n=1,\dots,N$, are linearly independent, so each bracket must vanish separately. Thus for each $n$,
$$
K_n(x) + \sum_{m=1}^N\frac{c_n^2e^{-(\chi_n+\chi_m)x}}{\chi_n+\chi_m}K_m(x) = -c_n^2e^{-\chi_nx}.
$$
For each fixed $x$ this is a linear system! Setting
\begin{align*}
	\vv c &= \left(c_1^2e^{-\chi_1x}, \dots, c_N^2e^{-\chi_Nx}\right)^T,\\
	\vv K &= \left(K_1(x),\dots,K_N(x)\right)^T,\\
	A_{nm} &= \delta_{nm} + \frac{c_n^2e^{-(\chi_n+\chi_m)x}}{\chi_n+\chi_m},
\end{align*}
the system reads
$$
A\vv K = -\vv c.
$$
So we have reduced an integral equation to inverting an $N\times N$ matrix. Even better, we do not need $\vv K$ itself, only the diagonal value
$$
K(x,x) = \sum_{m=1}^NK_m(x)e^{-\chi_mx} = \sum_{m,n}\left(A^{-1}\right)_{mn}\left(-\vv c\right)_ne^{-\chi_mx}.
$$
Now observe that
$$
A'_{nm}(x) = \frac{\dd}{\dd x}A_{nm}(x) = -c_n^2e^{-\chi_nx}e^{-\chi_mx} = \left(-\vv c\right)_ne^{-\chi_mx},
$$
which is exactly the combination appearing above. Hence
$$
K(x,x) = \sum_{m,n}\left(A^{-1}\right)_{mn}A'_{nm} = \tr\left(A^{-1}A'\right).
$$
Finally we use the identity (an exercise on the second example sheet, proved by expanding the determinant)
$$
\tr\left(A^{-1}A'\right) = \frac{1}{\det A}\frac{\dd}{\dd x}\left(\det A\right) = \frac{\dd}{\dd x}\log\left(\det A\right),
$$
which gives
$$
u(x) = -2\frac{\dd}{\dd x}K(x,x) = -2\frac{\dd^2}{\dd x^2}\log\left(\det A\right).
$$

Restoring the time dependence via $c_n(t)^2 = c_n(0)^2e^{8\chi_n^3t}$, we have proved the following.

\begin{theorem}[$N$-soliton solution]
	Let $\chi_1 > \cdots > \chi_N > 0$ and $c_1(0), \dots, c_N(0)$ be given. Then
	$$
	u(x,t) = -2\frac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2}\log\left(\det A(x,t)\right),
	$$
	where $A$ is the $N\times N$ matrix
	$$
	A_{nm}(x,t) = \delta_{nm} + \frac{c_n(0)^2e^{8\chi_n^3t}e^{-(\chi_n+\chi_m)x}}{\chi_n+\chi_m},
	$$
	is a solution of the KdV equation. It is the $N$-soliton solution, and describes $N$ solitary waves of amplitudes $2\chi_n^2$ and speeds $4\chi_n^2$.
\end{theorem}

We have obtained a closed-form solution to a nonlinear PDE, involving nothing worse than a determinant. Let us unpack it in the two simplest cases.

\begin{example}[$N = 1$]
	With a single bound state, $A$ is a $1\times1$ matrix,
	$$
	\det A = 1 + \frac{c_1(0)^2e^{8\chi_1^3t}e^{-2\chi_1x}}{2\chi_1} = 1 + e^{-2\chi_1(x - 4\chi_1^2t - \delta)},
	$$
	where the constant $\delta$ absorbs $c_1(0)$ via $e^{2\chi_1\delta} = c_1(0)^2/2\chi_1$. Writing $\xi = x - 4\chi_1^2t-\delta$ and $w = e^{-2\chi_1\xi}$,
	$$
	\log\det A = \log(1 + w),
	$$
	and differentiating twice (using $w' = -2\chi_1w$),
	$$
	\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\log(1+w) = \frac{-2\chi_1w}{1+w}, \qquad \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2}\log(1+w) = \frac{4\chi_1^2w}{(1+w)^2}.
	$$
	Hence
	$$
	u = -\frac{8\chi_1^2w}{(1+w)^2} = -\frac{8\chi_1^2}{\left(w^{1/2} + w^{-1/2}\right)^2} = -\frac{8\chi_1^2}{4\cosh^2(\chi_1\xi)} = -2\chi_1^2\sech^2\left(\chi_1(x - 4\chi_1^2t - \delta)\right),
	$$
	which is precisely the one-soliton solution of Section 2.3. So a single bound state means a single soliton, with $\chi_1$ determining both its height and its speed, and $c_1(0)$ determining only where it is.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[$N=2$]
	With two bound states, write
	$$
	E_n = E_n(x,t) = \frac{c_n(0)^2e^{8\chi_n^3t}e^{-2\chi_nx}}{2\chi_n},
	$$
	so that $A_{nn} = 1 + E_n$ and
	$$
	A_{12}A_{21} = \frac{c_1^2c_2^2e^{-2(\chi_1+\chi_2)x}}{(\chi_1+\chi_2)^2} = \frac{4\chi_1\chi_2}{(\chi_1+\chi_2)^2}E_1E_2.
	$$
	Therefore
	$$
	\det A = (1+E_1)(1+E_2) - \frac{4\chi_1\chi_2}{(\chi_1+\chi_2)^2}E_1E_2 = 1 + E_1 + E_2 + \left(\frac{\chi_2-\chi_1}{\chi_2+\chi_1}\right)^2E_1E_2,
	$$
	using $1 - \frac{4\chi_1\chi_2}{(\chi_1+\chi_2)^2} = \frac{(\chi_2-\chi_1)^2}{(\chi_2+\chi_1)^2}$. Substituting into the theorem gives the two-soliton solution plotted in Section 2.4.

	The interesting thing is what happens at large $|t|$, and this we can extract without any further computation. Write
	$$
	\delta = \frac{\chi_2-\chi_1}{\chi_2+\chi_1}\in(0,1),
	$$
	and follow the taller soliton by fixing $\xi = x - 4\chi_2^2t$. In this frame
	$$
	E_2 \propto e^{8\chi_2^3t - 2\chi_2(4\chi_2^2t+\xi)} = e^{-2\chi_2\xi},
	$$
	which is constant in $t$, while
	$$
	E_1\propto e^{8\chi_1^3t - 2\chi_1(4\chi_2^2t + \xi)} = e^{8\chi_1(\chi_1^2-\chi_2^2)t}e^{-2\chi_1\xi}\longrightarrow\begin{cases}0 & t\to+\infty,\\ \infty & t\to-\infty,\end{cases}
	$$
	since $\chi_1 < \chi_2$. So:
	\begin{itemize}
		\item As $t\to+\infty$ we have $\det A\to 1 + E_2$, and by the $N=1$ computation $u$ tends to a soliton of parameter $\chi_2$ sitting at $\xi = \delta_2$ for some fixed $\delta_2$.
		\item As $t\to-\infty$ the term $E_1$ dominates, and
		$$
		\det A \approx E_1\left(1 + \delta^2E_2\right).
		$$
		Now $\log E_1$ is a linear function of $x$, so it is killed by $\partial_x^2$ and contributes nothing. We are left with $u\to-2\partial_x^2\log(1+\delta^2E_2)$, which is again a soliton of parameter $\chi_2$ -- but with $E_2$ replaced by $\delta^2E_2$.
	\end{itemize}
	Since $E_2\propto e^{-2\chi_2\xi}$, multiplying $E_2$ by $\delta^2$ is the same as translating $\xi$ by
	$$
	\Delta \quad\text{where}\quad e^{2\chi_2\Delta} = \delta^2, \quad\text{i.e.}\quad \Delta = \frac{1}{\chi_2}\log\delta = -\frac{1}{\chi_2}\log\frac{\chi_2+\chi_1}{\chi_2-\chi_1} < 0.
	$$
	So long before the collision the tall soliton is at $\xi = \delta_2 + \Delta$, and long afterwards it is at $\xi = \delta_2$: it has been pushed \emph{forward} by
	$$
	|\Delta| = \frac{1}{\chi_2}\log\frac{\chi_2+\chi_1}{\chi_2-\chi_1}.
	$$
	Repeating the argument in the frame of the shorter soliton (where now $E_2$ is the one that blows up) gives a shift backwards by $\frac{1}{\chi_1}\log\frac{\chi_2+\chi_1}{\chi_2-\chi_1}$. These are the phase shifts we quoted in Section 2.4.
\end{example}

Two remarks are in order. First, the number of solitons is the number of bound states, so we can read off the eventual fate of \emph{any} initial profile from a linear eigenvalue problem: count the bound states of $-\partial_x^2 + u_0$, and that is how many solitons will emerge. Everything else -- the reflection coefficient -- disperses away, and one can show that it contributes a decaying oscillatory tail travelling in the opposite direction. So the general picture is:
\begin{center}
	\emph{any decaying initial profile resolves into $N$ solitons plus radiation.}
\end{center}
Second, the total absence of any interaction term in the final answer -- the shifts $\Delta$ depend only on $\chi_1$ and $\chi_2$, not on when or where the collision happened -- is precisely the sense in which solitons behave like particles.

\subsection{Infinitely Many Conservation Laws}

We promised at the start of Section 2 that an integrable PDE should have infinitely many conserved quantities, and in Section 2.5 we found three of them by inspection. Now we can produce them all at once.

The idea is that we have already found something conserved: the quantity $a(k,t) = a(k,0)$ is independent of time, for every $k$. If we can expand $a(k)$ in powers of $1/k$, then every coefficient in that expansion must be conserved as well, and we will have infinitely many first integrals in a single stroke.

Recall the setup: $\varphi = \varphi(x,k,t)$ solves $L\varphi = k^2\varphi$ with
$$
\varphi = \begin{cases}e^{-ikx} & x\to-\infty,\\ a(k,t)e^{-ikx} + b(k,t)e^{ikx} & x\to+\infty.\end{cases}
$$
Multiplying by $e^{ikx}$,
$$
e^{ikx}\varphi(x,k,t) = a(k) + b(k,t)e^{2ikx}\quad\text{as }x\to+\infty.
$$
To isolate $a$ we average out the oscillating term. Over the window $[R, 2R]$,
$$
\frac{1}{R}\int_R^{2R}a(k)\dd x = a(k), \qquad \frac{1}{R}\int_R^{2R}b(k,t)e^{2ikx}\dd x = O\!\left(\frac{1}{R}\right),
$$
so
$$
a(k) = \lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{R}\int_R^{2R}e^{ikx}\varphi(x,k,t)\dd x = \lim_{R\to\infty}\int_1^2e^{ikRx}\varphi(Rx,k,t)\dd x,
$$
substituting $x\mapsto Rx$.

Now we need a good representation of $\varphi$. Since $\varphi = e^{-ikx}$ as $x\to-\infty$, it is natural to write
$$
\varphi(x,k,t) = \exp\left(-ikx + \int_{-\infty}^xS(y,k,t)\dd y\right)
$$
for some function $S$. Feeding this into the formula above and passing to the limit,
\begin{equation*}
a(k) = \lim_{R\to\infty}\int_1^2\exp\left(\int_{-\infty}^{Rx}S(y,k,t)\dd y\right)\dd x = \exp\left(\int_{-\infty}^\infty S(y,k,t)\dd y\right).\tag{$\dagger$}
\end{equation*}
The left-hand side has no $t$ in it; the right-hand side appears to. That is where our conserved quantities will come from.

It remains to find $S$. Substituting the exponential ansatz into $L\varphi = k^2\varphi$, and using
$$
\varphi_x = (S-ik)\varphi, \qquad \varphi_{xx} = S_x\varphi + (S-ik)^2\varphi,
$$
we find, after dividing by $\varphi$,
$$
S_x - 2ikS + S^2 = -u.
$$
The function $\varphi$ has disappeared entirely, and we are left with a \vocab{Riccati equation} for $S$. (This is the Riccati equation of the Miura transform in disguise, which is no coincidence.)

We solve it as an asymptotic series in $1/k$:
$$
S(x,k,t) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{S_n(x,t)}{(2ik)^n}.
$$
Substituting and comparing coefficients of $k^{-n}$ gives the recursion
$$
S_1 = -u, \qquad S_{n+1} = \frac{\dd S_n}{\dd x} + \sum_{m=1}^{n-1}S_mS_{n-m}.
$$
This is entirely mechanical, and the first few terms are
$$
S_1 = -u, \qquad S_2 = -u_x, \qquad S_3 = -u_{xx} + u^2, \qquad S_4 = -u_{xxx} + 4uu_x, \ \dots
$$
Now put this back into $(\dagger)$:
$$
\log a(k) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty S(x,k,t)\dd x = \sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{1}{(2ik)^n}\int_{-\infty}^\infty S_n(x,t)\dd x.
$$
The left-hand side is independent of $t$, and this holds for all $k$, so every coefficient must be independent of $t$ separately. We conclude:

\begin{theorem}
	If $u$ evolves by KdV then, for every $n\ge1$,
	$$
	\int_{-\infty}^\infty S_n(x,t)\dd x
	$$
	is a first integral, where the $S_n$ are given by the recursion above.
\end{theorem}

Let us look at the first few.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
	\item $n=1$ gives
	$$
	\int_{-\infty}^\infty u\dd x,
	$$
	conservation of mass -- our $I_1$.
	\item $n=2$ gives $\int u_x\dd x$, which vanishes identically because $u$ decays. This is a \emph{trivial} conservation law: true, but empty. In fact one can show that $S_n$ is a total derivative for every even $n$, so the even members of the family give us nothing.
	\item $n=3$ gives
	$$
	\int_{-\infty}^\infty\left(-u_{xx} + u^2\right)\dd x = \int_{-\infty}^\infty u^2\dd x,
	$$
	conservation of momentum -- our $I_2$.
	\item $n=5$ gives $S_5 = -u_{xxxx} + 5u_x^2 + 6uu_{xx} - 2u^3$. Integrating, the first term vanishes and $\int 6uu_{xx}\dd x = -\int6u_x^2\dd x$ by parts, leaving
	$$
	\int_{-\infty}^\infty S_5\dd x = -\int_{-\infty}^\infty\left(u_x^2 + 2u^3\right)\dd x = -2I_3,
	$$
	so we recover the energy.
\end{enumerate}
So half of the family is trivial. But half of infinity is still infinity, and we have produced infinitely many genuine first integrals of KdV, exactly as the finite-dimensional theory led us to expect.

\begin{remark}
	It is worth noticing where the conserved quantities came from: they are the coefficients in an expansion of the transmission data. The scattering data $\{\chi_n\}$ and $R(k)$ are, in a real sense, the action variables of KdV; the phases of $c_n$ and $R$ are the angles, moving at constant rates $4\chi_n^3$ and $8k^3$. In the next section we make this analogy considerably more precise.
\end{remark}

\section{The Hamiltonian Structure of Integrable PDEs}

The integrable ODEs of Section 1 were not arbitrary ODEs -- they were Hamiltonian systems, and their integrability was expressed in terms of Poisson-commuting first integrals. If we really want to think of PDEs as infinite-dimensional dynamical systems, we should ask whether they too are Hamiltonian, and whether the conserved quantities we found are in involution. They are, and putting the theory in this language explains where the infinite family of conservation laws comes from.

\subsection{Infinite-Dimensional Hamiltonian Systems}

Recall the finite-dimensional set-up: a phase space $\R^{2n}$, a non-degenerate antisymmetric matrix $J$, a Hamiltonian $H : \R^{2n}\to\R$, and the equation of motion
$$
\frac{\dd\vv x}{\dd t} = J\frac{\partial H}{\partial \vv x}.
$$
We now promote each ingredient in turn. The rule of thumb is that the discrete index $i$ becomes the continuous variable $x$, sums become integrals, and partial derivatives become functional derivatives.

First, the inner product. In finite dimensions we had $\vv x\cdot\vv y = \sum_ix_iy_i$; here we replace the sum by an integral.

\begin{notation}
	For functions $u, v$ on $\R$ we write
	$$
	\ip{u}{v} = \int_\R u(x)v(x)\dd x.
	$$
	If $u$ and $v$ also depend on $t$, then so does the inner product.
\end{notation}

Secondly, functions on phase space. In finite dimensions the Hamiltonian was a function $H(\vv x)$; here the analogue is not a function of $u$ at each point, but a \emph{functional}.

\begin{definition}[Functional]
	A \vocab{functional} is a real-valued map on a space of functions of the form
	$$
	F[u] = \int_\R f(x, u, u_x, u_{xx}, \dots)\dd x.
	$$
	The function $f$ is called the \vocab{density}.
\end{definition}

Thirdly, differentiation with respect to phase-space coordinates. We met this in IB Variational Principles.

\begin{definition}[Functional derivative]
	The \vocab{functional derivative} (or Euler--Lagrange derivative) of $F$ at $u$ is the unique function $\delta F$ with
	$$
	\ip{\delta F}{\eta} = \lim_{\varepsilon\to0}\frac{F[u+\varepsilon\eta] - F[u]}{\varepsilon}
	$$
	for all smooth $\eta$ of compact support; equivalently
	$$
	F[u + \varepsilon\eta] = F[u] + \varepsilon\ip{\delta F}{\eta} + o(\varepsilon).
	$$
\end{definition}

Note that $\delta F$ is itself a function of $x$, depending on $u$. We often write $\delta F/\delta u$ for it.

\begin{example}
	Let $F[u] = \frac{1}{2}\int u_x^2\dd x$. Then
	\begin{align*}
		F[u+\varepsilon\eta] &= \frac{1}{2}\int\left(u_x + \varepsilon\eta_x\right)^2\dd x\\
		&= \frac{1}{2}\int u_x^2\dd x + \varepsilon\int u_x\eta_x\dd x + o(\varepsilon)\\
		&= F[u] + \varepsilon\ip{u_x}{\eta_x} + o(\varepsilon).
	\end{align*}
	This is not yet of the required form, since we want $\eta$ rather than $\eta_x$ in the second slot. When in doubt, integrate by parts -- and since $\eta$ has compact support there are no boundary terms:
	$$
	F[u+\varepsilon\eta] = F[u] + \varepsilon\ip{-u_{xx}}{\eta} + o(\varepsilon).
	$$
	So $\delta F = -u_{xx}$.
\end{example}

The general formula, from IB Variational Principles, is
$$
\delta F = \frac{\partial f}{\partial u} - \D_x\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial u_x}\right) + \D_x^2\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial u_{xx}}\right) - \cdots,
$$
where $\D_x$ is the \emph{total} derivative, not the partial one.

\begin{definition}[Total derivative]
	For a function $f(x, u, u_x, \dots)$ and a given $u = u(x)$, the \vocab{total derivative} is
	$$
	\D_xf = \frac{\partial f}{\partial x} + u_x\frac{\partial f}{\partial u} + u_{xx}\frac{\partial f}{\partial u_x} + \cdots.
	$$
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
	For $f = xu$ we have $\partial f/\partial x = u$ but $\D_xf = u + xu_x$. The partial derivative treats $u$ as an independent coordinate; the total derivative remembers that $u$ is a function of $x$.
\end{example}

Finally we need an analogue of $J$. It is helpful first to notice that in finite dimensions we can think of $J$ in two ways: as an antisymmetric bilinear form, or -- since we also have a dot product -- as an antisymmetric linear \emph{map}, applied via $\vv v\cdot J\vv w$. It is the second picture that generalises: we look for an antisymmetric linear operator $\mathcal J$ acting on functions, and define
$$
\{F, G\} = \ip{\delta F}{\mathcal J\delta G} = \int\delta F(x)\,\mathcal J\delta G(x)\dd x.
$$
Bilinearity and antisymmetry are then automatic. The Jacobi identity, however, is \emph{not}: it constrains the choice of $\mathcal J$.

\begin{definition}[Hamiltonian operator]
	A \vocab{Hamiltonian operator} is a linear, antisymmetric operator $\mathcal J$ on functions such that the induced bracket
	$$
	\{F,G\} = \ip{\delta F}{\mathcal J\delta G}
	$$
	satisfies the Jacobi identity.
\end{definition}

The simplest antisymmetric operator one can write down is $\mathcal J = \partial_x$ (antisymmetric because $\ip{u_x}{v} = -\ip{u}{v_x}$ after integrating by parts). One can check -- the proof is straightforward but tedious -- that it is Hamiltonian.

\begin{definition}[Hamiltonian form]
	An evolution equation for $u(x,t)$ is in \vocab{Hamiltonian form} if it can be written
	$$
	u_t = \mathcal J\frac{\delta H}{\delta u}
	$$
	for some functional $H[u]$ and some Hamiltonian operator $\mathcal J$.
\end{definition}

Everything from Section 1 now goes through verbatim.

\begin{proposition}
	If $u_t = \mathcal J\delta H$ and $I = I[u]$ is a functional, then
	$$
	\frac{\dd I}{\dd t} = \{I, H\}.
	$$
	In particular $I$ is a first integral if and only if $\{I, H\} = 0$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
	Directly from the definition of the functional derivative,
	$$
	\frac{\dd I}{\dd t} = \lim_{\varepsilon\to0}\frac{I[u+\varepsilon u_t] - I[u]}{\varepsilon} = \ip{\delta I}{u_t} = \ip{\delta I}{\mathcal J\delta H} = \{I, H\}. \qedhere
	$$
\end{proof}

In summary, the dictionary is:
\begin{center}
	\begin{tabular}{ll}
		\emph{$2n$-dimensional phase space} & \emph{infinite-dimensional phase space}\\[2pt]
		$x_i(t)$, $i = 1,\dots,2n$ & $u(x,t)$, $x\in\R$\\
		$\vv x\cdot\vv y = \sum_ix_iy_i$ & $\ip{u}{v} = \int u v\dd x$\\
		$\dd/\dd t$ & $\partial/\partial t$\\
		$\partial/\partial\vv x$ & $\delta/\delta u$\\
		antisymmetric matrix $J$ & antisymmetric operator $\mathcal J$\\
		functions $f(\vv x)$ & functionals $F[u]$\\
	\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{Bi-Hamiltonian Systems}

So far this is only a change of language. The interesting phenomenon is that the \emph{same} equation may be Hamiltonian in more than one way.

\begin{definition}[Bi-Hamiltonian]
	An evolution equation is \vocab{bi-Hamiltonian} if it can be written in Hamiltonian form for two different Hamiltonian operators,
	$$
	u_t = \mathcal J_0\delta H_0 = \mathcal J_1\delta H_1.
	$$
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[KdV is bi-Hamiltonian]
	Take
	$$
	\mathcal J_1 = \partial_x, \qquad H_1[u] = \int\left(\tfrac{1}{2}u_x^2 + u^3\right)\dd x.
	$$
	Then the density is $f = \frac{1}{2}u_x^2 + u^3$, and
	$$
	\delta H_1 = \frac{\partial f}{\partial u} - \D_x\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial u_x}\right) = 3u^2 - u_{xx},
	$$
	so
	$$
	\mathcal J_1\delta H_1 = \partial_x\left(3u^2 - u_{xx}\right) = 6uu_x - u_{xxx} = u_t,
	$$
	which is KdV. So $H_1$ -- which is precisely the first integral $I_3$ from Section 2.5 -- is the Hamiltonian for KdV.

	But KdV is also Hamiltonian with respect to a completely different structure. Take
	$$
	\mathcal J_0 = -\partial_x^3 + 4u\partial_x + 2u_x, \qquad H_0[u] = \int\tfrac{1}{2}u^2\dd x.
	$$
	Now $\delta H_0 = u$, and
	$$
	\mathcal J_0\delta H_0 = -u_{xxx} + 4uu_x + 2u_xu = -u_{xxx} + 6uu_x = u_t,
	$$
	which is KdV again. Here $H_0 = \frac{1}{2}I_2$ is the momentum. One checks that $\mathcal J_0$ is antisymmetric (the operators $\partial_x^3$ and $u\partial_x + \partial_xu$ both are) and that it satisfies the Jacobi identity, which is less obvious.
\end{example}

Why does this matter? Because two compatible Hamiltonian structures generate an infinite hierarchy of conserved quantities, all in involution, for free. Given the two structures, define a sequence of functionals $H_0, H_1, H_2,\dots$ recursively by
$$
\mathcal J_1\delta H_{n+1} = \mathcal J_0\delta H_n.
$$
(One has to show that this can always be solved for $H_{n+1}$; we take this on faith.) Then:

\begin{theorem}[Magri]
	Suppose $u_t = \mathcal J_0\delta H_0 = \mathcal J_1\delta H_1$ is bi-Hamiltonian, and let $\{H_n\}_{n\ge0}$ be defined by the recursion above. Then all the $H_n$ are first integrals, and they are in involution:
	$$
	\{H_n, H_m\} = 0\quad\text{for all }n,m\ge0,
	$$
	where the bracket is taken with respect to $\mathcal J_1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
	The whole proof rests on one observation. For $m\ge1$,
	\begin{align*}
		\{H_n, H_m\} &= \ip{\delta H_n}{\mathcal J_1\delta H_m}\\
		&= \ip{\delta H_n}{\mathcal J_0\delta H_{m-1}} && \text{(recursion)}\\
		&= -\ip{\mathcal J_0\delta H_n}{\delta H_{m-1}} && \text{($\mathcal J_0$ antisymmetric)}\\
		&= -\ip{\mathcal J_1\delta H_{n+1}}{\delta H_{m-1}} && \text{(recursion)}\\
		&= \ip{\delta H_{n+1}}{\mathcal J_1\delta H_{m-1}} && \text{($\mathcal J_1$ antisymmetric)}\\
		&= \{H_{n+1}, H_{m-1}\}.
	\end{align*}
	So we may shift an index from one slot to the other. Iterating $m$ times gives
	$$
	\{H_n, H_m\} = \{H_{n+m}, H_0\},
	$$
	and iterating $n$ times in the other direction gives $\{H_n,H_m\} = \{H_0, H_{n+m}\}$. Hence $\{H_n, H_m\} = \{H_m, H_n\}$; but the bracket is antisymmetric, so both sides vanish.

	Finally, taking $m = 1$ shows $\{H_n, H_1\} = 0$ for all $n$, and $H_1$ is the Hamiltonian, so every $H_n$ is a first integral.
\end{proof}

This is a genuinely miraculous piece of bookkeeping: the mere existence of a second Hamiltonian structure forces infinitely many commuting conservation laws into existence. For KdV the resulting $H_n$ are (up to normalisation) the integrals $\int S_{2n+1}\dd x$ we constructed from the scattering problem in Section 3.6. And the flows they generate,
$$
u_{t_n} = \mathcal J_1\delta H_n,
$$
are the members of the KdV hierarchy: $n=0$ gives $u_t = u_x$ (translation), $n=1$ gives KdV, $n=2$ gives a fifth-order equation, and so on. Since the Hamiltonians are in involution, all these flows commute, exactly as the Hamiltonian flows on a Liouville torus did in Section 1.

\subsection{Zero-Curvature Representation}

There is a second, more geometric way to package integrability, which is often more flexible than the Lax formulation.

Given a function $u(x,t)$, suppose we can construct $N\times N$ matrices $U = U(\lambda)$ and $V = V(\lambda)$ depending on $\lambda$, on $u$ and on its derivatives. Here $\lambda$ is a \vocab{spectral parameter}, playing the same role as the $\lambda$ in $L\varphi = \lambda\varphi$. Consider the linear system
\begin{equation*}
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\vv v = U(\lambda)\vv v, \qquad \frac{\partial}{\partial t}\vv v = V(\lambda)\vv v,\tag{$\dagger$}
\end{equation*}
for an $N$-component vector $\vv v = \vv v(x,t;\lambda)$.

This is an overdetermined system -- we have twice as many equations as unknowns -- so there is a compatibility condition, obtained from $\vv v_{xt} = \vv v_{tx}$:
\begin{align*}
	0 &= \frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(U\vv v\right) - \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(V\vv v\right)\\
	&= U_t\vv v + U\vv v_t - V_x\vv v - V\vv v_x\\
	&= U_t\vv v + UV\vv v - V_x\vv v - VU\vv v\\
	&= \left(U_t - V_x + [U,V]\right)\vv v.
\end{align*}
So if $(\dagger)$ is to have a solution for every initial $\vv v_0$, we need

\begin{definition}[Zero-curvature equation]
	The \vocab{zero-curvature equation} is
	$$
	\frac{\partial U}{\partial t} - \frac{\partial V}{\partial x} + [U, V] = 0.
	$$
\end{definition}

A theorem of Frobenius says the converse also holds: if the zero-curvature equation is satisfied, then solutions of $(\dagger)$ exist. So we have a correspondence between the solvability of a linear system and a nonlinear equation for $U, V$.

The name comes from differential geometry. The curvature of a connection $A$ has components
$$
F_{ij} = \frac{\partial A_j}{\partial x_i} - \frac{\partial A_i}{\partial x_j} + [A_i, A_j],
$$
which is exactly the shape of our equation with $(A_1, A_2) = (U, V)$ and $(x_1, x_2) = (x, t)$. So the equation says that the connection built from $U$ and $V$ is flat.

\begin{example}[Sine--Gordon]
	Take
	$$
	U(\lambda) = \frac{i}{2}\begin{pmatrix}2\lambda & u_x\\ u_x & -2\lambda\end{pmatrix}, \qquad V(\lambda) = \frac{1}{4i\lambda}\begin{pmatrix}\cos u & -i\sin u\\ i\sin u & -\cos u\end{pmatrix}.
	$$
	A computation shows that the zero-curvature equation is equivalent to
	$$
	u_{xt} = \sin u,
	$$
	the sine--Gordon equation in light-cone coordinates (Section 5.1).
\end{example}

The geometric picture pays off immediately. Curvature is an intrinsic object: if it vanishes in one gauge it vanishes in all of them. So given one pair $(U, V)$ solving the zero-curvature equation, a gauge transformation
$$
U\mapsto \tilde U = gUg^{-1} + g_xg^{-1}, \qquad V\mapsto\tilde V = gVg^{-1} + g_tg^{-1}
$$
produces a new pair solving the same equation, and hence potentially a new integrable equation, or new solutions of the same one. This is explored on the last example sheet.

Zero curvature also supports its own version of inverse scattering. In the sine--Gordon example above, if $u_x\to0$ as $|x|\to\infty$ then writing $\vv v = (\psi_1,\psi_2)^T$ the first equation of $(\dagger)$ becomes
$$
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_1\\\psi_2\end{pmatrix} = i\lambda\begin{pmatrix}\psi_1\\-\psi_2\end{pmatrix},
$$
so that
$$
\begin{pmatrix}\psi_1\\\psi_2\end{pmatrix}\to A\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}e^{i\lambda x} + B\begin{pmatrix}0\\1\end{pmatrix}e^{-i\lambda x}
$$
as $|x|\to\infty$. The coefficients $A$ and $B$ are the scattering data, and the second equation of $(\dagger)$ tells us how they evolve in $t$. This is the AKNS scheme, and it is how one solves sine--Gordon and the nonlinear Schr\"odinger equation.

\subsection{From Lax Pairs to Zero Curvature}

The two formulations are closely related, and in fact every Lax pair gives rise to a zero-curvature representation.

Recall that in the isospectral flow theorem we found two equations holding simultaneously:
$$
L\psi = \lambda\psi, \qquad \psi_t + A\psi = 0,
$$
the second because $\tilde\psi$ vanished. Conversely, suppose we \emph{impose} these two equations and demand $\lambda_t = 0$. Differentiating the first and substituting the second gives
$$
L_t\psi + L\psi_t = \lambda\psi_t \implies L_t\psi = (L - \lambda)(-\psi_t) = (L-\lambda)A\psi = LA\psi - AL\psi,
$$
that is $L_t = [L,A]$. So Lax's equation is exactly the compatibility condition for the pair of linear equations above -- the same logical role played by the zero-curvature equation for $(\dagger)$.

To make the correspondence explicit, suppose
$$
L = \partial_x^n + \sum_{j=0}^{n-1}u_j(x,t)\partial_x^j, \qquad A = \partial_x^m + \sum_{j=0}^{m-1}v_j(x,t)\partial_x^j.
$$
The equation $L\psi = \lambda\psi$ lets us express every derivative of order $\ge n$ in terms of the lower ones:
$$
\partial_x^n\psi = \lambda\psi - \sum_{j=0}^{n-1}u_j\partial_x^j\psi,
$$
and differentiating this repeatedly handles the higher derivatives. So introducing the vector
$$
\bm\Psi = \left(\psi, \partial_x\psi, \dots, \partial_x^{n-1}\psi\right)^T,
$$
the equation $L\psi = \lambda\psi$ becomes a first-order system $\bm\Psi_x = U(\lambda)\bm\Psi$ with companion matrix
$$
U(\lambda) = \begin{pmatrix}
	0 & 1 & 0 & \cdots & 0\\
	0 & 0 & 1 & \cdots & 0\\
	\vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\
	0 & 0 & 0 & \cdots & 1\\
	\lambda - u_0 & -u_1 & -u_2 & \cdots & -u_{n-1}
\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Similarly, differentiating $\psi_t + A\psi = 0$ a total of $i-1$ times with respect to $x$ and using the above to eliminate high derivatives, we get
$$
\left(\partial_x^{i-1}\psi\right)_t + \sum_{j=1}^nV_{ij}(x,t)\partial_x^{j-1}\psi = 0
$$
for suitable $V_{ij}$ built from the $u_j, v_j$ and their derivatives, that is $\bm\Psi_t = -V\bm\Psi$ (after an overall sign). So
$$
L_t = [L,A]
\iff
\begin{cases}L\psi = \lambda\psi\\ \psi_t + A\psi = 0\end{cases}
\iff
\begin{cases}\bm\Psi_x = U(\lambda)\bm\Psi\\ \bm\Psi_t = V(\lambda)\bm\Psi\end{cases}
\iff
U_t - V_x + [U,V] = 0.
$$
The zero-curvature formulation is strictly more general -- there are integrable equations with a zero-curvature representation but no scalar Lax pair -- and it is the one that generalises most readily to other settings.

\section{Further Integrable Equations}

KdV is not alone. In this section we meet two more equations which are integrable in the same sense -- they have Lax pairs, infinitely many conservation laws and soliton solutions -- and which are at least as important in applications.

\subsection{The Sine--Gordon Equation}

\begin{definition}[Sine--Gordon equation]
	The \vocab{sine--Gordon equation} is
	$$
	u_{tt} - u_{xx} + \sin u = 0.
	$$
\end{definition}

The name is a joke. The \vocab{Klein--Gordon equation} of relativistic quantum mechanics is
$$
u_{tt} - u_{xx} + u = 0,
$$
and replacing the $u$ by $\sin u$ turns Klein into sine.

\begin{example}[A chain of pendulums]
	Here is a mechanical system governed by the equation. Take a horizontal rod, and hang from it a row of pendulums of length $\ell$ and mass $m$, spaced $\Delta x$ apart, each free to rotate in the vertical plane perpendicular to the rod. Let $\theta_i(t)$ be the angle of the $i$th pendulum from the downward vertical, and connect neighbouring masses by torsional springs.
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzpicture}
			\draw[thick] (0,0) -- (5.5,0);
			\foreach \x/\a in {0/20, 1/-10, 2/35, 3/5, 4/-25, 5/15} {
				\draw (\x, 0) -- ++({\a-90}:1) node[circle, fill=blue, inner sep=1.6pt] {};
			}
			\draw[latex-latex] (0,0.35) -- (1,0.35) node[pos=0.5, above, font=\scriptsize] {$\Delta x$};
			\draw[latex-latex] (-0.45,0) -- (-0.45,-1) node[pos=0.5, left, font=\scriptsize] {$\ell$};
			\node[font=\scriptsize] at (5.26,-1.3) {$m$};
		\end{tikzpicture}
	\end{center}
	Gravity exerts a torque $-m\ell g\sin\theta_i$ on the $i$th pendulum, and the springs exert torques
	$$
	\frac{K(\theta_{i+1}-\theta_i)}{\Delta x}, \qquad \frac{K(\theta_{i-1}-\theta_i)}{\Delta x}
	$$
	from its two neighbours. Newton's second law for rotation, with moment of inertia $m\ell^2$, gives
	$$
	m\ell^2\frac{\dd^2\theta_i}{\dd t^2} = -mg\ell\sin\theta_i + \frac{K\left(\theta_{i+1} - 2\theta_i + \theta_{i-1}\right)}{\Delta x}.
	$$
	Now divide by $\Delta x$ and take the continuum limit $\Delta x\to0$, holding $M = m/\Delta x$ fixed and writing $\theta_i(t) = \theta(i\Delta x, t)$. The second difference over $\Delta x^2$ tends to $\theta_{xx}$, so
	$$
	M\ell^2\frac{\partial^2\theta}{\partial t^2} = -Mg\ell\sin\theta + K\frac{\partial^2\theta}{\partial x^2}.
	$$
	Rescaling $x$ and $t$ to remove the constants gives $u_{tt} - u_{xx} + \sin u = 0$.
\end{example}

There is also a differential-geometric origin: solutions of the sine--Gordon equation correspond to \emph{pseudospherical} surfaces in $\R^3$, that is surfaces of constant negative Gaussian curvature. This is the historical source of the equation, and of the B\"acklund transformations of Section 6.

It is often convenient to pass to \vocab{light-cone coordinates}
$$
\xi = \tfrac{1}{2}(x - t), \qquad \tau = \tfrac{1}{2}(x+t),
$$
in which $\partial_x = \frac{1}{2}(\partial_\xi + \partial_\tau)$ and $\partial_t = \frac{1}{2}(\partial_\tau - \partial_\xi)$, so that $\partial_t^2 - \partial_x^2 = -\partial_\xi\partial_\tau$. The equation becomes
$$
\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial\xi\partial\tau} = \sin u,
$$
which is the form we will mostly use.

\subsubsection{Kinks and Antikinks}

Let us find the travelling waves. Put $u(x,t) = f(\gamma(x - vt))$ with $|v|<1$ and $\gamma = (1-v^2)^{-1/2}$, and write $\theta = \gamma(x-vt)$. Then $u_{tt} = v^2\gamma^2f''$ and $u_{xx} = \gamma^2f''$, so
$$
\gamma^2(v^2-1)f'' + \sin f = 0, \qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad f'' = \sin f,
$$
since $\gamma^2(v^2-1) = -1$. This is the equation of a pendulum with the sign of gravity reversed -- which is no coincidence, given where the equation came from. Multiplying by $f'$ and integrating,
$$
\tfrac{1}{2}(f')^2 = -\cos f + C,
$$
and imposing $f\to0$, $f'\to0$ as $\theta\to-\infty$ gives $C = 1$. Hence
$$
(f')^2 = 2(1-\cos f) = 4\sin^2(f/2), \qquad f' = 2\sin(f/2),
$$
taking the positive root. Separating variables and substituting $g = f/2$,
$$
\int\frac{\dd g}{\sin g} = \int\dd\theta \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \log\tan\frac{g}{2} = \theta + \text{const},
$$
so $\tan(f/4) = Ae^{\theta}$. Absorbing $A$ into a translation we obtain the following.

\begin{proposition}[Kink solution]
	For any $|v|<1$,
	$$
	u(x,t) = 4\tan^{-1}\left(\exp\left(\frac{x - vt}{\sqrt{1-v^2}}\right)\right)
	$$
	solves the sine--Gordon equation. It is called a \vocab{kink}. Replacing $x - vt$ by $-(x-vt)$ gives the \vocab{antikink}.
\end{proposition}

The kink interpolates between $u = 0$ at $x=-\infty$ and $u = 2\pi$ at $x=+\infty$; the antikink runs the other way.

\end{center}
Note the Lorentz factor $\gamma$: a fast kink is narrower than a slow one, exactly as a moving rod is contracted in special relativity. This is not an accident, since the sine--Gordon equation is Lorentz invariant.

In the pendulum picture, a kink is a single full twist of the chain, propagating along it. Now remember that $u$ is an \emph{angle}: the values $0$ and $2\pi$ describe the same physical configuration. So both the kink and the trivial solution $u=0$ satisfy the same boundary conditions in the physical sense. Nevertheless the kink can never evolve into the trivial solution, and here is why.

\begin{definition}[Topological charge]
	For a solution with $u\to 2\pi n_\pm$ as $x\to\pm\infty$, the \vocab{topological charge} is
	$$
	Q = \frac{1}{2\pi}\left[u\right]_{x=-\infty}^{x=+\infty} = n_+ - n_-\in\Z.
	$$
\end{definition}

Since $u$ evolves continuously and $Q$ takes values in $\Z$, the charge cannot change: it is conserved for purely topological reasons, with no differential equation required. The kink has $Q = 1$, the antikink $Q = -1$, and the vacuum $Q = 0$. So a kink is stable, not because it sits at a minimum of some energy, but because there is no continuous path from it to anything else.

If we think of $u$ as taking values on the circle $S^1$, the picture is that the solution is a path in the cylinder $\R\times S^1$ which winds $Q$ times around:
\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.62]
		\draw (-5.5,1.5) -- (5.5,1.5);
		\draw (-5.5,0) -- (5.5,0);
		\draw (-5.5,0.75) ellipse (0.3 and 0.75);
		\draw (5.5,1.5) arc (90:-90:0.3 and 0.75);
		\draw[dashed] (5.5,1.5) arc (90:270:0.3 and 0.75);
		\draw[thick, color=blue] (-5.5,0.04) to[out=4,in=185] (-1.4,0.22) to[out=5,in=205] (1.0,1.2) to[out=25,in=175] (2.3,1.48);
		\draw[thick, color=blue, dashed] (2.3,1.48) to[out=-5,in=95] (3.0,0.75) to[out=275,in=175] (3.7,0.04);
		\draw[thick, color=blue] (3.7,0.04) to[out=-5,in=185] (5.5,0.04);
		\node[font=\scriptsize, left] at (-5.85,0.75) {$u \in S^1$};
		\node[font=\scriptsize, right] at (5.9,0.75) {$x$};
	\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
No amount of continuous deformation, subject to the boundary conditions, can unwind it.

\begin{remark}
	Sine--Gordon has multi-soliton solutions too, obtained (as we shall see in Section 6) by B\"acklund transformations. Besides kink--kink and kink--antikink scattering solutions there is a genuinely new object, the \vocab{breather}
	$$
	u(x,t) = 4\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\sqrt{1-\omega^2}}{\omega}\,\frac{\sin(\omega t)}{\cosh\left(\sqrt{1-\omega^2}\,x\right)}\right),
	$$
	a kink and antikink bound together, oscillating in place with frequency $\omega$. It has $Q = 0$, and yet it is perfectly stable -- a periodic, localised, nonlinear excitation with no linear analogue.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The Nonlinear Schr\"odinger Equation}

Our final example is the equation governing envelope waves: pulses in optical fibres, wave packets on deep water, and Bose--Einstein condensates.

\begin{definition}[Nonlinear Schr\"odinger equation]
	The \vocab{nonlinear Schr\"odinger equation} (NLS) for a complex-valued $q(x,t)$ is
	$$
	iq_t + q_{xx} + 2|q|^2q = 0.
	$$
\end{definition}

The sign of the nonlinear term matters. With $+2|q|^2q$ the equation is \vocab{focusing}, and supports \emph{bright} solitons -- localised pulses on a zero background. With $-2|q|^2q$ it is \vocab{defocusing}, and supports \emph{dark} solitons -- localised dips in a non-zero background. We treat the focusing case.

The equation looks like the linear Schr\"odinger equation of IB Quantum Mechanics with a potential $-2|q|^2$ generated by the wavefunction itself, which is exactly the physical situation in a nonlinear optical medium whose refractive index depends on the intensity of the light passing through it.

Let us find the soliton. Look first for a standing solution
$$
q(x,t) = f(x)e^{i\theta t}
$$
with $f$ real. Then $iq_t = -\theta fe^{i\theta t}$, and the equation becomes
$$
f'' = \theta f - 2f^3.
$$
Multiplying by $f'$ and integrating, with $f, f'\to0$ at infinity,
$$
(f')^2 = \theta f^2 - f^4 = f^2\left(\theta - f^2\right).
$$
This has exactly the shape of the KdV travelling-wave equation, and the same guess works. Trying $f = a\sech(ax)$ we get
$$
(f')^2 = a^4\sech^2(ax)\tanh^2(ax) = a^4\sech^2 - a^4\sech^4,
$$
while
$$
f^2(\theta - f^2) = a^2\theta\sech^2 - a^4\sech^4,
$$
so the two agree precisely when $\theta = a^2$.

Finally we set the soliton moving. NLS is Galilean invariant: if $q(x,t)$ is a solution then so is
$$
\tilde q(x,t) = e^{i\left(\frac{v}{2}x - \frac{v^2}{4}t\right)}q(x - vt, t),
$$
as one checks by direct substitution. Applying this to the standing solution gives the following.

\begin{proposition}[Bright soliton]
	For any $a > 0$ and $v\in\R$,
	$$
	q(x,t) = a\sech\left(a(x-vt)\right)\exp\left(i\left[\frac{v}{2}x + \left(a^2 - \frac{v^2}{4}\right)t\right]\right)
	$$
	solves the focusing NLS equation.
\end{proposition}

So the modulus $|q| = a\sech(a(x-vt))$ is a solitary wave of amplitude $a$ and width $1/a$, travelling at speed $v$, while the phase oscillates underneath it:

\end{center}
Note the crucial difference from KdV: here the amplitude and the speed are \emph{independent} parameters. A tall NLS soliton is narrow, but it need not be fast.

Like KdV, the NLS equation has a Lax pair and is solvable by inverse scattering; the relevant linear problem is the $2\times2$ Zakharov--Shabat system,
$$
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_1\\\psi_2\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}-i\lambda & q\\ -\bar q & i\lambda\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_1\\\psi_2\end{pmatrix},
$$
which is exactly of the zero-curvature type $\bm\Psi_x = U(\lambda)\bm\Psi$ discussed in Section 4.3. Each discrete eigenvalue of this system corresponds to a soliton, with the real and imaginary parts of the eigenvalue giving the soliton's amplitude and velocity respectively. NLS solitons, like KdV solitons, pass through one another with nothing but a phase shift to show for it.

Finally, NLS is a Hamiltonian system in the sense of Section 4.1, with
$$
iq_t = \frac{\delta H}{\delta\bar q}, \qquad H[q] = \int\left(|q_x|^2 - |q|^4\right)\dd x,
$$
and it has the conserved quantities
$$
\int|q|^2\dd x, \qquad \frac{i}{2}\int\left(q\bar q_x - \bar q q_x\right)\dd x, \qquad H[q]
$$
-- the number of photons, the momentum, and the energy -- together with infinitely many more.
